# `assessCompetence` — step by step

**Location:** `aura-code-src/src/archimedes/competence.ts:141-202`. It answers one question: *should this task go to the small local model (Archimedes) or be escalated to the large model?* It never throws — every failure path returns a decision.

## The walkthrough

**1. Sanitize config (line 147 → `safeConfig`, 34-48).**
Every field is defaulted and clamped: `competenceThreshold` forced into 0–1, `minAttempts` floored at 0. Defaults come from `types.ts:68-76` — `competenceThreshold: 0.7`, `minAttempts: 3`, `enabled: true`. So a missing or garbage config can't produce a nonsense gate.

**2. Kill switch (150-157).** If `enabled` is false → `useArchimedes: false`, confidence 1, escalate to `claude-sonnet-4-5-20251001`. This is the only branch that ignores history entirely.

**3. Match history (159-166).**
- `safeEpisodes` (90-95) drops anything that isn't an object with a string `task` and a boolean `archimedesAttempted`.
- `matched` keeps episodes whose task is "similar" to the current one (`tasksSimilar`, 71-78): exact match, substring either way, or Jaccard token overlap ≥ 0.35 on tokens longer than 2 chars.
- `pattern` is a key like `implementation:fix_the_login_bug` — first 8 tokens, prefixed by the category of the *first* match (or `other`).
- `buildLevelFromEpisodes` (97-119) then computes the stats — **and this is the subtle part**: it filters to `archimedesAttempted` only. Episodes where Archimedes was skipped don't count as attempts. `attemptCount` = number of real attempts, `successRate` = successes / attempts (0 when no attempts), `examples` = last 10, `lastUpdated` = newest timestamp.

**4. Branch A — below `minAttempts` (168-176).**
`attemptCount < cfg.minAttempts` (default: fewer than 3 attempts).
- **Decision: `useArchimedes: true`** — the small model gets the task anyway.
- **Why:** deliberate cold-start policy. With no track record there's nothing to judge, and the only way the score ever moves is by actually attempting. Gating here would freeze the pattern at zero forever.
- **Confidence:** `0.3 + (attemptCount / minAttempts) * 0.3` → 0.30 at zero attempts, rising to 0.60 just under the threshold. Low confidence, but a real attempt.
- Note the asymmetry: this branch fires on **attempt count**, not success rate. A pattern with 2 attempts and 2 failures still goes to Archimedes.

**5. Branch B — at/above `minAttempts`, success rate clears the bar (178-186).**
`successRate >= competenceThreshold` (default 0.7).
- **Decision: `useArchimedes: true`**, confidence `successRate * min(1, attemptCount/10)` — so 0.7 rate at 3 attempts gives 0.21 confidence, at 10+ attempts gives 0.7. Trust grows with sample size, not just the ratio.

**6. Branch C — at/above `minAttempts`, success rate below the bar (188-194).**
- **Decision: `useArchimedes: false`** — escalate to the large model, confidence `(1 - successRate) * min(1, attemptCount/10)`. The worse Archimedes has done, the more confident the escalation.

**7. Catch-all (195-201).** Any thrown error → `useArchimedes: true`, confidence 0, reason "Assessment error". Fail-open toward the cheap model, but flagged with zero confidence.

## Below vs. above `minAttempts` — the contrast

| | Below `minAttempts` | At/above `minAttempts` |
|---|---|---|
| What's consulted | attempt count only | success rate vs. `competenceThreshold` |
| Outcome | always `useArchimedes: true` | true if rate ≥ threshold, false otherwise |
| Confidence | 0.30 → 0.60, ramping with attempts | rate-scaled, capped by `attemptCount/10` |
| Purpose | gather training data | enforce the competence gate |

The threshold is a **data-collection floor, not a quality gate**. Below it, failure history is ignored on purpose; above it, failure history is the only thing that matters.

## Two things worth flagging

- **`epsilonProbeRate` is not used in this function.** `types.ts:58-64` documents it as the mechanism that keeps a gated pattern's score alive — without it, a pattern that falls below threshold is never attempted again, so its score can never recover. That probing must live in the caller (`alternator.ts` imports `assessCompetence` and `shouldFineTune`); `assessCompetence` itself returns a hard `false` for gated patterns. If the caller doesn't apply epsilon, the gate is permanent.
- **`pattern` is computed but only used in the reason strings.** The gate itself keys off `matched` (the similarity-filtered episode list), not off the pattern key. So two tasks that are similar but land on different pattern slugs still share a gate decision — which is the intended behaviour, but it means the pattern in the message is descriptive, not the lookup key.

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

**`updateCompetence`** — `aura-code-src/src/archimedes/competence.ts:212-250`

It's a running (cumulative) average, not a decayed or windowed one. Step by step:

1. **Guard** (line 217): if the episode wasn't attempted by Archimedes (`!episode.archimedesAttempted`), it returns a copy of the existing list untouched — no attempt, no rate change.
2. **Find the bucket** (lines 220-222): the pattern key comes from `taskPatternFrom(episode.task, episode.taskCategory)`; it looks up the matching `CompetenceLevel` in the list.
3. **Reconstruct prior successes** (line 230): `prevSuccesses = Math.round(prev.successRate * prev.attemptCount)` — the stored rate is the only persisted state, so successes are recovered by multiplying it back out.
4. **Add this episode** (lines 229-231): `attemptCount = prev.attemptCount + 1`, `newSuccesses = prevSuccesses + (archimedesSucceeded ? 1 : 0)`.
5. **New rate** (line 232): `successRate = newSuccesses / attemptCount`, then `clamp01` on write (line 236).

So the formula is:

```
rate_new = (round(rate_old × attempts_old) + [success ? 1 : 0]) / (attempts_old + 1)
```

For a brand-new pattern, `prev` is undefined → `attemptCount = 1`, `prevSuccesses = 0`, so the rate is exactly `1` or `0`.

Two consequences worth knowing:
- **It's a lifetime average.** A pattern that failed 20 times and then succeeds 20 times sits at 0.5 — old failures never age out. Only `examples` is bounded (last 10, line 227); the rate itself has no window.
- **Rounding drift.** Because successes are re-derived from a float rate each call, `Math.round` can lose or gain a success over long histories (e.g. rate 0.333… × 3 → 1, not 1.0 exactly). The error is bounded at ±0.5 success, so it's cosmetic, but it means the rate isn't bit-exact reproducible from the episode log — `getCompetenceReport` (line 260) recomputes from raw episodes and can disagree slightly with the stored map.

The whole body is wrapped in `try/catch` (lines 216, 247) that returns the previous list on any error, so it never throws.

Here's how **`getCompetenceReport`** works:

### Grouping performance
The function groups episodes **by `taskCategory`** — specifically, it uses a `Map<string, { successes: number; count: number }>()` keyed on the category extracted from each episode (`ep.taskCategory ?? 'other'`). For every episode that has an attempt recorded and is not null:

- It increments the bucket's **`count`** by 1.
- If `ep.archimedesSucceeded` is true, it also increments **`successes`** by 1.

So each entry in the final result represents one task category (or "other" if no category is set) and aggregates how many episodes fell into that category and how many succeeded within them.

### Sorting of resulting records
After mapping to `{ category, successRate, count }`, the array is sorted with:

```js
.sort((a, b) => b.count - a.count);
```

This sorts **descending by `count`** — i.e., categories/records with more episodes come first. Within ties (same count), the original order from the map iteration determines relative placement.

### Summary of return shape
Each record is an object:
```js
{
  category: string,
  successRate: number,   // successes / count (clamped to 0 if count === 0)
  count: number
}
```

Sorted by `count` descending.

I found the `saveEpisode` method in `/aura-code-src/src/archimedes/episode-capture.ts`. Here's the atomic write pattern:

```typescript
async saveEpisode(projectRoot: string, episode: Episode): Promise<void> {
    const dir = this.projectDir(projectRoot);
    if (!fs.existsSync(dir)) fs.mkdirSync(dir, { recursive: true });
    const filePath = this.episodePath(projectRoot, episode.id);
    const tmp = filePath + '.tmp';
    await fs.promises.writeFile(tmp, JSON.stringify(episode, null, 2), 'utf8');
    await fs.promises.rename(tmp, filePath);
}
```

### How the atomic write works (3 steps):

1. **Create target directory** — `mkdirSync(dir)` ensures the project's episode folder exists.
2. **Write to a temporary file** — Instead of overwriting directly, it writes to `filePath + '.tmp'` using `writeFile`. This produces an intermediate state with only partial/new content on disk.
3. **Atomically rename (move) the temp file over the target** — `fs.promises.rename(tmp, filePath)` performs a single OS-level atomic move.

### Why this prevents corrupted episode files:

- **No partial writes**: If `writeFile` fails mid-stream, only the `.tmp` file exists — there is no half-written or partially serialized JSON on disk.
- **`fs.rename()` is atomic** (on POSIX systems): It's a single metadata operation that either fully succeeds or raises an error. There is never an intermediate state where both `filePath` and `tmp` exist with inconsistent content. The filesystem guarantees the move completes in one step, so no reader can see a torn file.
- **Namespaced by project hash** (see `projectHash()`): Each project's episodes live under `.aura/episodes/{projectHash}/`, keeping writes isolated and preventing cross-contamination.

This mirrors the pattern used elsewhere for sessionStore — writing to a `.tmp` buffer first, then atomically replacing it.

  ### How the atomic write works (3 steps):

  2. **Write to a temporary file** — Instead of overwriting directly, it writes to `filePath + '.tmp'` using `writeFile`. This produces an intermediate state with only partial/new content on disk.
  3. **Atomically rename (move) the temp file over the target** — `fs.promises.rename(tmp, filePath)` performs a single OS-level atomic move.

  - **`fs.rename()` is atomic** (on POSIX systems): It's a single metadata operation that either fully succeeds or raises an error. There is never an intermediate state where both `filePath` and `tmp` exist with inconsistent content. The filesystem guarantees the move completes in one step, so no reader can see a torn file.

# How `loadEpisodes` handles corrupt / invalid-JSON files

`loadEpisodes` is defined at `aura-code-src/src/archimedes/episode-capture.ts:64-92` (re-exported as a bound function at line 143).

**The short answer: it skips them silently and keeps going. It never throws, and it never repairs or deletes anything.**

## The three layers of protection

**1. Per-file parse guard (lines 74-81)** — the important one.
Each file is read and `JSON.parse`'d inside its own `try`. If the JSON is malformed, the `catch` swallows it with the comment `/* skip corrupt file */` and the loop moves to the next file. One bad file cannot abort the load of the other 999.

**2. Shape validation (line 76)** — a file that parses but isn't a valid episode is also dropped:
```ts
if (parsed?.id && typeof parsed.timestamp === 'number') episodes.push(parsed);
```
So `null`, `[]`, `{}`, a truncated-but-valid object, or an episode missing `id`/`timestamp` is discarded. Note this is a *minimal* check — it does not validate the rest of the `Episode` fields (`archimedesAttempted`, `largeModelUsed`, etc.), so a file with a valid `id` and `timestamp` but garbage elsewhere will be loaded and passed downstream.

**3. Outer catch (lines 89-91)** — covers directory-level failures: unreadable dir, permission error, `readdirSync` throwing, or a file deleted between `readdirSync` and `readFile` (a real race, since the read is `await`ed inside the loop). Any of those returns `[]` for the whole project.

## Consequences worth knowing

- **Silent data loss.** A corrupt file is invisible: no log, no counter, no warning. `getEpisodeStats` (line 107) will simply report a lower `total`, and `readyForFineTune` is computed from the surviving subset — so corruption can quietly skew the fine-tune decision.
- **Corrupt files are never cleaned up.** They stay on disk forever and are re-read and re-rejected on every single call. Cost is O(n) file reads per load, with the bad ones paying a full read + failed parse each time.
- **Partial writes are already prevented upstream.** `saveEpisode` (lines 51-58) writes to `.tmp` then `rename`s, which is atomic on the same filesystem — so a crash mid-write leaves a `.tmp` file, not a truncated `.json`. The `.json` filter on line 69 also excludes those `.tmp` leftovers. Corruption therefore comes from external causes (disk, manual edits, older non-atomic writers), not from this module.
- **`limit` is applied after the full load** (line 85-87), so a corrupt file still costs a read even when you only wanted the newest 10.

## If you want it to be less silent

The minimal change is to count and report skips rather than swallow them — e.g. collect `skipped: string[]` and expose it, or `console.warn` the filename in the inner `catch`. I haven't made that change since you asked how it behaves, not for a fix — say the word and I'll add it.

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

## `getEpisodeStats` — what it returns and how it counts

**Location:** `aura-code-src/src/archimedes/episode-capture.ts:107-138` (interface at `:14-20`)

### The five fields

| Field | How it's computed | Line |
|---|---|---|
| `total` | `episodes.length` — every episode file that loaded successfully | `:123` |
| `archimedesSuccesses` | episodes where `archimedesAttempted && archimedesSucceeded` | `:116` |
| `archimedesFailures` | episodes where `archimedesAttempted && !archimedesSucceeded` | `:117` |
| `largeModelInterventions` | episodes where `largeModelUsed` is truthy | `:119` |
| `readyForFineTune` | delegated to `shouldFineTune(episodes)` | `:127` |

### Successes vs. interventions — the key distinction

These are **two independent counters over the same episode list**, not two sides of one split:

- **Successes/failures are gated on `archimedesAttempted`.** An episode where Archimedes was never invoked (routed straight to the large model) increments *neither* counter. So `archimedesSuccesses + archimedesFailures ≤ total`, and the gap is exactly the number of episodes Archimedes was bypassed on.
- **Interventions are gated on `largeModelUsed` only** — no attempt check. An episode counts as an intervention whenever a large model produced output, whether that was because Archimedes failed, was skipped, or was disabled entirely.

Consequence: `largeModelInterventions` is **not** the same as `archimedesFailures`. Interventions ≥ failures in general, because bypassed episodes add to interventions without adding to failures. The two only coincide when every episode attempted Archimedes.

### Semantics of "success"

Per `types.ts:100-101`, `archimedesSucceeded` means Archimedes's output was **accepted without large-model intervention** — so a success is by definition an episode with no escalation. The counters are therefore mutually exclusive per episode: an attempted episode lands in exactly one of successes or failures, never both.

### `readyForFineTune`

`shouldFineTune` (`competence.ts:299-313`) recomputes the failure count itself — `archimedesAttempted && !archimedesSucceeded` — and returns true at **≥ 20 failures** (`DEFAULT_MIN_FAILURES`, `competence.ts:17`). It does not read the `archimedesFailures` field; it re-derives from the same episode array, so the two always agree. Note the doc comment at `:294-295` describes a failure as "large model intervened **or** reviewer rejected", but the code only checks `!archimedesSucceeded` — `reviewerApproved` is never consulted. The code is the truth here; the comment overstates it.

### Robustness

Both `loadEpisodes` (`:64-92`) and `getEpisodeStats` (`:108-137`) are wrapped in try/catch and never throw: a missing directory yields `[]`, corrupt JSON files are skipped silently (`:79-81`), and any error returns all-zero stats with `readyForFineTune: false`. Episodes are sorted newest-first by `timestamp` (`:84`), but since `getEpisodeStats` calls `loadEpisodes` with no limit, the sort has no effect on the counts — it's O(n log n) work whose result is discarded.

### Callers

`alternator.ts:524` and `orchestration/strategy.ts:154` both call it per project root; episodes are namespaced by the first 8 chars of the base64 of `projectRoot` (`:34-36`), so stats are always scoped to one project.

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

  ### Successes vs. interventions — the key distinction
  
  These are **two independent counters over the same episode list**, not two sides of one split:
  
  - **Successes/failures are gated on `archimedesAttempted`.** An episode where Archimedes was never invoked (routed straight to the large model) increments *neither* counter. So `archimedesSuccesses + archimedesFailures ≤ total`, and the gap is exactly the number of episodes Archimedes was bypassed on.
  - **Interventions are gated on `largeModelUsed` only** — no attempt check. An episode counts as an intervention whenever a large model produced output, whether that was because Archimedes failed, was skipped, or was disabled entirely.
  
  Consequence: `largeModelInterventions` is **not** the same as `archimedesFailures`. Interventions ≥ failures in general, because bypassed episodes add to interventions without adding to failures. The two only coincide when every episode attempted Archimedes.

  Per `types.ts:100-101`, `archimedesSucceeded` means Archimedes's output was **accepted without large-model intervention** — so a success is by definition an episode with no escalation. The counters are therefore mutually exclusive per episode: an attempted episode lands in exactly one of successes or failures, never both.

  `shouldFineTune` (`competence.ts:299-313`) recomputes the failure count itself — `archimedesAttempted && !archimedesSucceeded` — and returns true at **≥ 20 failures** (`DEFAULT_MIN_FAILURES`, `competence.ts:17`). It does not read the `archimedesFailures` field; it re-derives from the same episode array, so the two always agree. Note the doc comment at `:294-295` describes a failure as "large model intervened **or** reviewer rejected", but the code only checks `!archimedesSucceeded` — `reviewerApproved` is never consulted. The code is the truth here; the comment overstates it.

  Both `loadEpisodes` (`:64-92`) and `getEpisodeStats` (`:108-137`) are wrapped in try/catch and never throw: a missing directory yields `[]`, corrupt JSON files are skipped silently (`:79-81`), and any error returns all-zero stats with `readyForFineTune: false`. Episodes are sorted newest-first by `timestamp` (`:84`), but since `getEpisodeStats` calls `loadEpisodes` with no limit, the sort has no effect on the counts — it's O(n log n) work whose result is discarded.

**It isn't real fine-tuning — it's prompt injection baked into a derived model.**

`fineTuneWithOllama()` (`aura-code-src/src/archimedes/fine-tune.ts:29-99`) fakes it with a Modelfile:

1. **Reads the JSONL training file** (line 39-41) and counts lines as `trainingExamples`.
2. **Extracts up to 50 examples** (line 44) — for each row it pulls the `user` and `assistant` messages and flattens them into a plain text snippet `"User: …\nAssistant: …"` (lines 46-51). Malformed lines are silently skipped (line 52-54).
3. **Builds a system prompt** (lines 57-61): a fixed preamble ("You are a specialized coding assistant. Learn from these corrected examples where a larger model intervened:") followed by all the snippets concatenated.
4. **Writes a Modelfile** (lines 63-72): `FROM <baseModel>` plus that text inside a `SYSTEM """…"""` block, with any embedded `"""` escaped to `'''` so the Modelfile can't be broken by the data.
5. **Calls `ollama create <outputModelName> -f Modelfile`** (line 74, 5-minute timeout). Ollama just copies the base weights and attaches the system prompt as model metadata — no weights change.
6. **Returns a `FineTuneJob`** with `status: 'completed'` and `outputModel: outputModelName` (lines 78-86), or `status: 'failed'` with the error string (lines 87-98). It never throws.

So the "fine-tuned" model is the base model wearing a long system prompt of up to 50 corrected examples. Two consequences worth knowing: the examples are only in the system prompt, so they eat context window on every call, and only the first 50 rows ever influence behaviour — the rest are counted in `trainingExamples` but discarded. Contrast with `fineTuneWithOpenAI()` (line 109+), which does a genuine upload + `fineTuning.jobs.create` and polls real job status (line 210+).

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

  2. **Extracts up to 50 examples** (line 44) — for each row it pulls the `user` and `assistant` messages and flattens them into a plain text snippet `"User: …\nAssistant: …"` (lines 46-51). Malformed lines are silently skipped (line 52-54).
  3. **Builds a system prompt** (lines 57-61): a fixed preamble ("You are a specialized coding assistant. Learn from these corrected examples where a larger model intervened:") followed by all the snippets concatenated.
  4. **Writes a Modelfile** (lines 63-72): `FROM <baseModel>` plus that text inside a `SYSTEM """…"""` block, with any embedded `"""` escaped to `'''` so the Modelfile can't be broken by the data.
  5. **Calls `ollama create <outputModelName> -f Modelfile`** (line 74, 5-minute timeout). Ollama just copies the base weights and attaches the system prompt as model metadata — no weights change.
  6. **Returns a `FineTuneJob`** with `status: 'completed'` and `outputModel: outputModelName` (lines 78-86), or `status: 'failed'` with the error string (lines 87-98). It never throws.
  
  So the "fine-tuned" model is the base model wearing a long system prompt of up to 50 corrected examples. Two consequences worth knowing: the examples are only in the system prompt, so they eat context window on every call, and only the first 50 rows ever influence behaviour — the rest are counted in `trainingExamples` but discarded. Contrast with `fineTuneWithOpenAI()` (line 109+), which does a genuine upload + `fineTuning.jobs.create` and polls real job status (line 210+).

# `fineTuneWithOpenAI` — walkthrough

**Location:** `aura-code-src/src/archimedes/fine-tune.ts:109-164`

**Signature:** `fineTuneWithOpenAI(trainingDataPath: string, baseModel = 'gpt-3.5-turbo'): Promise<FineTuneJob>`

## Step by step

1. **Key check (lines 113-127)** — `getApiKey('OPENAI_API_KEY')`. If missing, it returns a `failed` job immediately with `error: 'OPENAI_API_KEY is not set'`. No network call is made. The function never throws; every failure path returns a `FineTuneJob`.

2. **Count examples (lines 130-131)** — reads the JSONL file locally and counts non-blank lines. This is only for the `trainingExamples` field in the result; the file is read a second time as a stream for upload.

3. **Client construction (line 133)** — `new OpenAI({ apiKey })`, default base URL (`api.openai.com/v1`).

4. **Client call #1 — upload the file (lines 134-137)**
   ```ts
   client.files.create({ file: fs.createReadStream(trainingDataPath), purpose: 'fine-tune' })
   ```
   → `POST /v1/files` (multipart). Returns a `file` object; only `file.id` is used.

5. **Client call #2 — create the job (lines 139-142)**
   ```ts
   client.fineTuning.jobs.create({ training_file: file.id, model: baseModel })
   ```
   → `POST /v1/fine_tuning/jobs`. Note it passes only `training_file` and `model` — no `hyperparameters`, no `suffix`, no `validation_file`, no `integrations`.

6. **Return (lines 144-151)** — `id: 'openai-' + job.id` (the prefix is how `checkJobStatus` later routes the job), `status: 'running'`, `outputModel: job.fine_tuned_model ?? ''` (normally empty at creation time — the model name only appears once training succeeds).

7. **Error path (lines 152-163)** — any throw from either call is caught and returned as a `failed` job with `String(e)` as the error.

## So: exactly two client calls

| # | Call | HTTP |
|---|------|------|
| 1 | `client.files.create({ file, purpose: 'fine-tune' })` | `POST /v1/files` |
| 2 | `client.fineTuning.jobs.create({ training_file, model })` | `POST /v1/fine_tuning/jobs` |

## The third call lives elsewhere

`fineTuneWithOpenAI` does **not** poll. Progress is picked up by `checkJobStatus` (`:174`), which routes on the `openai-` id prefix to `checkOpenAIJobStatus` (`:210`), and that makes the third call:

- `client.fineTuning.jobs.retrieve(openaiId)` → `GET /v1/fine_tuning/jobs/{id}` (`:224`), with the remote status mapped `validating_files|queued → pending`, `running → running`, `succeeded → completed`, `failed|cancelled → failed` (`:226-233`).

## Two things worth flagging

- **No `purpose` validation or format check** before upload — a malformed JSONL is only rejected server-side, surfacing as a `failed` job with the raw API error string.
- **`trainingExamples` is `0` on any failure** (line 157), even when the file was read successfully and the failure happened at upload/job creation. The count is computed but discarded on the error path.

**Four lifecycle states**, declared at `aura-code-src/src/archimedes/types.ts:170`:

```ts
status: 'pending' | 'running' | 'completed' | 'failed';
```

| State | Meaning | Where it's set |
|---|---|---|
| `pending` | Submitted but not yet training — maps from OpenAI's `validating_files` and `queued` | `fine-tune.ts:227-228` (status map) |
| `running` | Training in progress | `fine-tune.ts:146` (OpenAI job created), `fine-tune.ts:229` |
| `completed` | Terminal success; `outputModel` holds the resulting model tag | `fine-tune.ts:80` (Ollama), `fine-tune.ts:202`, `fine-tune.ts:230` |
| `failed` | Terminal error; `error` field carries the message | `fine-tune.ts:90`, `119`, `155`, `192`, `204`, `206`, `215`, `231-232`, `246` |

Notes on the semantics:
- **Terminal states are `completed` and `failed`** — `checkOllamaJobStatus` short-circuits on them (`fine-tune.ts:188`), and `completedAt` is only stamped when a job reaches one (`fine-tune.ts:236-242`).
- **`cancelled` is not a separate state** — OpenAI's `cancelled` is folded into `failed` (`fine-tune.ts:232`).
- **`pending` is never produced by the local Ollama path** — `fineTuneWithOllama` runs `ollama create` synchronously and returns `completed` or `failed` directly (`fine-tune.ts:74-98`). Only the OpenAI path can report `pending`.
- Unknown remote statuses fall back to the job's current status (`fine-tune.ts:235`), so no fifth state can leak in.

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

  - **Terminal states are `completed` and `failed`** — `checkOllamaJobStatus` short-circuits on them (`fine-tune.ts:188`), and `completedAt` is only stamped when a job reaches one (`fine-tune.ts:236-242`).

I'll answer this directly — it's a diagnostic question, not a code change.

## Exit code 137 = 128 + 9 → the process was killed by **SIGKILL**

The container didn't crash on its own. Something sent signal 9, and SIGKILL can't be caught, so the process died instantly with no cleanup, no stack trace, no error log.

### The two realistic causes

**1. The OOM killer (most common by far)**

The kernel's out-of-memory killer picked that process and killed it. Two distinct flavours:

- **Container memory limit hit** — the cgroup limit (`docker run -m 512m`, or `mem_limit` / `deploy.resources.limits.memory` in Compose) was exceeded. The kernel kills a process inside that cgroup. This is the classic 137.
- **Host memory exhausted** — no container limit set, so the container ate the whole host's RAM and the host OOM killer chose it. Same exit code, worse blast radius.

How to tell them apart:
```bash
docker inspect <container> --format '{{.State.OOMKilled}}'   # true = OOM
docker inspect <container> --format '{{.HostConfig.Memory}}' # 0 = no limit set
dmesg -T | grep -i -E 'killed process|out of memory'         # kernel's own record
```

**2. `docker stop` / orchestrator timeout**

`docker stop` sends SIGTERM, waits 10s (`--time`), then SIGKILL. If your app ignores SIGTERM or takes longer than the grace period to shut down, you get 137. Same for Kubernetes `terminationGracePeriodSeconds`, ECS, Swarm, and CI runners tearing down jobs. This one is *not* a memory problem — check whether the exit coincides with a deploy, scale-down, or job timeout.

### Ruling out the impostors

- **137 from `docker run` itself** (not the container) usually means the *docker CLI* was killed — e.g. a CI job timeout killing the runner.
- **Exit 143** = 128 + 15 = SIGTERM, a clean-ish stop. If you see 143 you're fine; 137 means the grace period expired.
- **Exit 139** = SIGSEGV, a real crash. Different problem.

### Fixing it

If it's OOM:
- Raise the limit only after measuring — `docker stats` and `docker inspect` peak usage. Blindly bumping it just moves the wall.
- Find the actual leak or the unbounded buffer: JVM heap (`-Xmx` must be *below* the cgroup limit, or the JVM ignores the limit and gets killed), Node `--max-old-space-size`, Python workers × per-worker RSS, or a query loading a whole table into memory.
- Set limits *deliberately* so the kill is contained to one container instead of the host.

If it's a stop timeout:
- Handle SIGTERM in the app and exit within the grace period.
- Raise `stop_grace_period` / `terminationGracePeriodSeconds` if shutdown legitimately needs longer.

**The one-line answer:** 137 means SIGKILL — either the OOM killer enforcing a memory limit, or a stop/termination grace period that expired. `docker inspect --format '{{.State.OOMKilled}}'` tells you which within a second.

────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

This is a classic symptom of one of three things — let me walk through the most likely causes:

## Most Likely Causes

### 1. **Rate Limiting / Throttling (most common)**
The API enforces rate limits per IP/key and returns `401 Unauthorized` on the *first* request after a burst, or when the limit window hasn't reset yet. A short wait (2 seconds) often lets the server's internal cooldown expire before processing your next call.

### 2. **Session Token Expiration / Refresh**
The initial call uses an old/invalid session token. The first request fails with `401`, but after waiting, the server may issue a fresh token or refresh the existing one on subsequent calls (depending on implementation).

### 3. **Caching Header Invalidation**
Some APIs return `401` for cached responses that haven't been invalidated yet; waiting allows cache expiration to take effect.

---

**To pinpoint the exact cause, I'd need:**
- The API endpoint URL
- Whether you're using an auth header (e.g., Bearer token)
- Any rate-limit headers in responses

# Non-fast-forward rejection: what happened and what you can do

## What happened

Git refused the push because your branch's history and the remote's history have **diverged**. The remote branch has commits your local branch doesn't have, so accepting your push would have *discarded* them. Git's rule is that a push must be a pure fast-forward — your new tip must be a descendant of the remote tip. It isn't, so it aborted.

The usual causes, in order of likelihood:

1. **Someone else pushed to the same branch** (or you pushed from another machine/another clone). Most common by far.
2. **You amended or rebased commits that were already pushed** — rewriting history that the remote still has in its old form.
3. **You reset backwards** (`git reset --hard HEAD~n`) after pushing.
4. **The remote branch was force-pushed** by someone else, so your base is stale.

The message itself tells you which: `Updates were rejected because the tip of your current branch is behind its remote counterpart` means the remote is ahead (case 1/4). `...because the remote contains work that you do not have locally` is the same situation. If it says `non-fast-forward` after a rebase/amend, it's case 2.

## Your options

**1. Integrate the remote work — the default, safe answer.**
```
git fetch origin
git log --oneline HEAD..origin/<branch>   # what they have that you don't
git log --oneline origin/<branch>..HEAD   # what you have that they don't
git pull --rebase origin <branch>         # or: git merge origin/<branch>
git push
```
Rebase gives a linear history and is what most teams want for a feature branch; merge preserves the true topology and is safer on a shared branch. Either way, nothing is destroyed and the push then fast-forwards.

**2. Force-push — only when you *know* the remote commits are yours and disposable.**
```
git push --force-with-lease origin <branch>
```
`--force-with-lease` refuses if the remote moved since your last fetch, so it won't silently clobber a colleague's push. Plain `--force` will. Never force-push `main`/`master` or any branch others build on without explicit agreement — that's the one genuinely destructive option here.

**3. Push to a different branch and open a PR.**
```
git push origin HEAD:refs/heads/my-feature
```
Zero risk, no history rewriting, and it's the right move when you're unsure whether the divergence is yours to resolve.

**4. Abandon your local commits** (only if they're wrong): `git reset --hard origin/<branch>` — this *does* delete your unpushed work, so stash or branch it first.

## Recommendation

Fetch, inspect both sides of the divergence, then `git pull --rebase` and push. That resolves ~95% of these. Reach for `--force-with-lease` only after confirming the remote-only commits are ones you personally created and no longer want.

One caveat specific to this workspace: `/tmp/aura-bench/session_11017/t2-04` is **not a git repository** (no `.git` anywhere up the tree), so I can't inspect your actual divergence or run the resolution here — the above is the general diagnosis plus the exact commands to run in the real clone.

Great question — I can point out the most common reasons a function unexpectedly returns `undefined` without needing to inspect your specific codebase:

### 1. Missing or absent `return` statement
The simplest cause: if the function body ends without an explicit `return`, Python (and many other languages) implicitly return `None`/`undefined`. A developer might think they're "passing through" a value, but nothing gets returned.

**Fix:** Add a `return <value>` before or at the end of the function.

### 2. Undefined / unbound variables inside the function
If you reference a variable (e.g., `x`, `self`, `request`) that hasn't been assigned yet — especially in async code where initialization happens later, or on early returns/throws — Python will raise an error *before* execution; but if it's evaluated lazily or the scope is different, the function may silently return `undefined`.

**Fix:** Ensure all referenced variables are defined at call time.

### 3. Calling before the function is properly initialized / async race condition
In asynchronous code (e.g., callbacks, event loops), if a function is invoked before its setup phase completes — or if it's called with stale arguments/state — it may receive incomplete context and return `undefined`.

**Fix:** Guarantee initialization order, use proper awaiting patterns, or add defensive checks.

**Answer: `claude-sonnet-4-5-20251001`**

It's defined at **`aura-code-src/src/archimedes/competence.ts:14`**:

```ts
/** Large model used when Archimedes is not trusted or is disabled. */
export const DEFAULT_FALLBACK_MODEL = 'claude-sonnet-4-5-20251001';
```

**Important nuance — that constant is the declared default, but it is not what actually runs.** At runtime the fallback is the *user's configured large model*, not the hardcoded string:

- `aura-code-src/src/archimedes/alternator.ts:295` and `:315` — the alternator overwrites the decision's `fallbackModel` with `largeModelProvider.model`, i.e. whatever provider/model the user actually configured. So if Archimedes is bypassed or disabled, the task goes to the user's own primary model.
- `aura-code-src/src/archimedes/types.ts:136` — `AlternationDecision.fallbackModel` is the field carrying it.

Two related-but-distinct fallback mechanisms, so they aren't confused with this one:

- `aura-code-src/src/config/defaults.ts:79` — `FALLBACK_CHAIN` is **empty by default**, deliberately: hardcoding vendor models there silently sent traffic to providers the user had no keys for. Set via `--fallback`, `AURA_FALLBACK_MODEL`, or `"fallbacks"` in `.aura.json`.
- `aura-code-src/src/providers/fallback.ts:9` — `FallbackChainProvider` implements that chain.

So: the *documented* Archimedes fallback is `claude-sonnet-4-5-20251001` (competence.ts:14), but the *effective* one is the configured large model (alternator.ts:315).

I have the full picture. Here's the exact contract, straight from `aura-code-src/src/archimedes/alternator.ts`.

## Expected verifier response format

The verifier is a **single `complete()` call** to the large model — no tools, no history, one system message and one user message (lines 235–239). The system prompt says: *"You are a strict verifier. Judge only whether the proposed answer addresses the task. Reply with exactly one line."*

The user prompt ends with the hard requirement (line 231):

```
Reply with exactly one line: either "VALID" or "INVALID: <short reason>".
```

So the expected output is **one line**, in one of two shapes:
- `VALID`
- `INVALID: <short reason>`

The prompt also instructs the verifier to cross-check the answer against the summarized tool results and to mark **fabrications** INVALID — e.g. a tool said a symbol/file wasn't found but the answer describes it in detail (lines 224–230).

## How failures are parsed

Parsing is deliberately loose and fail-safe (lines 240–248):

```ts
const text = response.text.trim();
if (text.toUpperCase().startsWith('VALID')) {
  return { valid: true, reason: '' };
}
const reason = text.replace(/^INVALID:?\s*/i, '') || 'failed verification';
return { valid: false, reason };
```

Three rules, in order:

1. **Success is a prefix match, case-insensitive.** Anything whose trimmed, uppercased text *starts with* `VALID` passes — `VALID`, `valid`, `Valid.` all pass. Note the consequence: a reply like `VALID? No, it's wrong` would also pass, because only the prefix is checked.
2. **Everything else is a failure.** There is no explicit `INVALID` check — the code only tests for `VALID` and treats the remainder as invalid. So a refusal, an empty string, a rambling paragraph, or a malformed reply all count as **invalid**.
3. **The reason is the text with a leading `INVALID:` stripped** (regex `/^INVALID:?\s*/i`, so the colon is optional). If nothing remains, it falls back to the literal string `'failed verification'`.

**Exception path (line 246–248):** if the `complete()` call throws for any reason, the function returns `{ valid: false, reason: 'verification error: <error>' }`. The doc comment at lines 201–206 states the intent explicitly: *"Fail-safe: any verification error counts as invalid (escalate), never as silent trust."*

## What the caller does with it

The result type is `ArchimedesVerification { valid: boolean; reason: string }` (lines 162–165). In `run()` (lines 378–404):

- **`valid: true`** → Archimedes's output is accepted as the final result, `archimedesSucceeded`/`usedArchimedes` are set, and it logs "Archimedes handled the task without escalation."
- **`valid: false`** → the reason plus the tool-activity summary plus the rejected answer are packed into `archimedesFailureContext` and handed to the large model, which redoes the task independently (line 398 explicitly labels the rejected answer *"for reference only — verify independently"*).

One structural detail worth knowing: verification only runs if Archimedes produced a non-empty summary **and** `loopResult.success` was true (line 378). A failed or empty Archimedes run skips the verifier entirely and escalates with a different context message (lines 405–415).

I found the implementation in `alternator.ts`. Here's how it works:

### How `summarizeToolActivity` summarizes for the verifier

The function takes an array of `HistoryMessage[]` and produces a human-readable summary string (`lines.join('\n')`). It does three things:

1. **Builds an args map** — iterates over assistant messages that have `toolCalls`. For each tool call, it extracts `call.input`, stores the result keyed by `call.id` in `argsById`.
2. **Iterates over `tool_result` history entries** — for each result message (`msg.role === 'tool_result'`), loops over its `results` array.
3. **Formats and outputs lines** — builds a line like `- `<result.name>(<args>) -> <content>``.

### Truncation limit applied to each tool result

- **`MAX_RESULT_CHARS = 300`** (defined at line 175). On line 192–194, for every `tool_result.results[i].content`, the code does:
  ```ts
  let content = r.content.replace(/\s+/g, ' ').trim();
  if (content.length > MAX_RESULT_CHARS) {
    content = content.slice(0, MAX_RESULT_CHARS) + '…';
  }
  ```
- So **each tool result's `content` field is truncated to the first 300 characters** and suffixed with `…` if it exceeds that length.

*(Note: arguments from `call.input` are separately capped at 120 chars on line 181, but the question asks about each tool result — which uses the 300-char limit.)*

### Summary

| Step | Truncation target | Limit |
|------|-------------------|-------|
| Tool call input (`args`) | `JSON.stringify(call.input)` per call.id | **120 characters** (suffix `'…'` if longer) |
| Tool result content (`r.content`) | After whitespace normalization and trim | **300 characters** (suffix `'…'` if longer, via `MAX_RESULT_CHARS = 300`) |

The function returns the joined lines or `"(no tools were called)"` when none exist.

## Why tool results go into the verification prompt

**Because the final answer is the one thing in the transcript that cannot be checked against itself.**

The verifier's job is to answer "did this actually happen?" — and the final answer is a *claim*, not evidence. In this codebase the loop hands the verifier three things (`src/agent/loop.ts:90-101`):

- `history` — the full conversation, including every `tool_result` message pushed at `loop.ts:586`
- `toolCallLog` — every call made, recorded at `loop.ts:561`, commented literally as *"used by the verify layer"*
- `summary` / `success` — the agent's own self-report

If you sent only the final answer, you'd be asking a model to grade a student by reading the student's own essay about the exam. An agent that hallucinated "I fixed the bug and all tests pass" would produce a final answer that reads perfectly — and a verifier with no tool results would have nothing to contradict it. It would pass. That's the exact failure mode verification exists to catch.

Concretely, the tool results are what let the verifier catch:

1. **Claims with no supporting call.** "I ran the tests" — but `toolCallLog` has no `run_tests` entry. That's a fabrication, and it's invisible without the log.
2. **Claims contradicted by output.** "All 42 tests pass" — but the `run_tests` result in `history` says `3 failed`. The final answer and the tool output disagree; only the tool output is ground truth.
3. **Errors the agent swallowed.** `loop.ts:484-512` pushes `isError: true` results for blocked calls, user denials, and plugin-hook blocks. An agent can narrate straight past a `Blocked: ...` result and report success. The `isError` flag is the only record that it didn't happen.
4. **Truncated evidence.** `loop.ts:575-580` caps any tool result at 8,000 chars and appends `[result truncated: N chars total]`. A verifier reading the raw result knows the agent may have concluded from partial output — a nuance that vanishes entirely if you only pass the summary.

There's also a structural reason: the loop already *has* this data for free. `history` and `toolCallLog` are accumulated during the run and returned in `LoopResult` regardless. Passing them costs nothing extra; omitting them means the verifier is strictly less informed than the loop that produced the answer.

**The tradeoff, stated honestly:** tool results are bulky — up to 8K chars each, times every call in the run. That's why the 8K cap exists, and it's the real cost of this design. The mitigation is that the verifier gets *evidence*, not the whole transcript verbatim; a well-built verifier prompt selects the calls that bear on the claims being made rather than dumping everything.

## One thing you should know about this snapshot

`src/verify/` is **empty**. `src/agent/loop.ts:13` imports `VerificationConfig` from `../verify/types.js`, and `loop.ts:616-618` dynamically imports `runWithVerification` from `../verify/index.js` — but neither file exists here. So the verification prompt itself isn't in this tree; I answered from the loop side, which is where the tool results are collected and where the contract is declared (`loop.ts:99-100`).

If you want, I can write the missing `src/verify/` module — types, the prompt builder that selects relevant tool results, and `runWithVerification` — so the import at `loop.ts:617` actually resolves. Say the word and I'll build it.

**Default epsilon probe rate: `0.05` (5%)**, defined by the constant `DEFAULT_EPSILON_PROBE_RATE` at `aura-code-src/src/archimedes/alternator.ts:94`.

Details:
- **Constant:** `const DEFAULT_EPSILON_PROBE_RATE = 0.05;` — line 94, with a doc comment at lines 82–93 explaining the rationale.
- **What it does:** it's the probability of overriding a gated (`useArchimedes: false`) decision and letting Archimedes attempt anyway. Without it, once `assessCompetence` gates a task pattern below threshold, that pattern's score would never update again (since `archimedesAttempted` only flips inside the `decision.useArchimedes` branch) — the gate would become permanent. The probe keeps the score live.
- **Why so low:** each probe still pays the full Archimedes-then-large-model cost, so it isn't a free background check.
- **Override:** per project via `archimedes.epsilonProbeRate` in `.aura.json` — resolved in `aura-code-src/src/archimedes/competence.ts:44` (`clamp01(...)`), typed in `archimedes/types.ts:64` and `config/project-config.ts:58`.

I have everything needed. This is a question about existing code, so the answer is the deliverable — no code change is warranted.

## Why the epsilon probe rate is kept low

**The mechanism** (`aura-code-src/src/archimedes/alternator.ts:331-343`):

```ts
const epsilon = archimedesConfig.epsilonProbeRate ?? DEFAULT_EPSILON_PROBE_RATE; // 0.05
if (!decision.useArchimedes && archimedesConfig.enabled && Math.random() < epsilon) {
  decision = { ...decision, useArchimedes: true, reason: `[probe] Overriding gate...` };
}
```

The probe exists to solve a **frozen-gate deadlock**. `assessCompetence` (`competence.ts:178-194`) gates a task pattern once its success rate falls below `competenceThreshold` (default 0.7). But `archimedesAttempted` only becomes `true` inside the `decision.useArchimedes` branch (`alternator.ts:352`). So a gated pattern never produces another attempt → its score never updates → the gate is permanent even if the local model later improves. The probe is the only escape hatch, and `:small1` is the manual one.

It is kept at **0.05** (5%) rather than something higher because of what a probe actually costs.

## Cost implications

**A probe is not a cheap background check — it is the most expensive path in the alternator.** The doc comment at `alternator.ts:89-91` says this explicitly: *"the probe still pays full Archimedes-then-large-model cost on every trial (verification always runs)."*

Walking the code, a probe that fails (the expected outcome — that's *why* the pattern was gated) pays for **three** model calls:

1. **Archimedes agent loop** (`alternator.ts:357-373`) — up to `maxTurns: 15` turns against the local model. Free in dollars (Ollama/LM Studio), but not free in wall-clock: a 15-turn loop on a 1.5B model is the slowest thing in the run.
2. **Verifier call** (`alternator.ts:379-384` → `verifyArchimedesAnswer`, `:207-249`) — a **large-model** `complete()` with no tools. This is billed tokens on every single probe, success or failure. It is deliberately not a full agent loop, but it is still a paid call.
3. **Full large-model agent loop** (`alternator.ts:443-454`) — because the probe failed verification, `usedArchimedes` stays false and the whole task runs again on the large model, with the Archimedes failure context appended to the prompt (`:430-441`), which *inflates* the input tokens versus a clean run.

So the cost of a probe is roughly **`large-model full loop` + `verifier call` + `local loop latency`** — strictly more than just routing to the large model directly. A probe is a **negative-value bet on cost**: you pay a premium for the chance of proving the gate wrong.

**The arithmetic is what justifies 5%.** For a gated pattern, expected overhead per task is `epsilon × (verifier + wasted local loop + inflated large-model prompt)`. At 5%, one in twenty gated tasks pays the premium; the other nineteen go straight to the large model at baseline cost. At 50% you would double the cost of half your gated traffic to learn the same thing — and the information gained is nearly identical, because competence scores are computed from a rolling window of the last 50 episodes (`RECENT_EPISODE_LIMIT`, `alternator.ts:79`). You need only a handful of new samples to move a score; you do not need half your traffic.

**The probe is also self-limiting in the right direction.** It only fires when `!decision.useArchimedes` — a pattern still in its `minAttempts` learning phase (default 3) is already using Archimedes and needs no probe (`alternator.ts:334-336`). And once a probe succeeds, the score rises, the gate opens, and the pattern stops being probed at all. The 5% is a floor on exploration, not a steady-state tax.

**Two secondary costs worth naming:**

- **Latency, not just dollars.** A probe serialises local loop → verifier → large loop. The user waits for all three. At 5% that's an occasional slow task; at a high rate it would be the common case.
- **Verifier false-negatives are fail-safe but not free** (`alternator.ts:246-248`): any verification error counts as INVALID, so a flaky verifier call converts a probe into a guaranteed full large-model run. Low epsilon bounds how often that failure mode can bite.

**Tuning guidance:** raise `archimedes.epsilonProbeRate` in `.aura.json` (`config/project-config.ts:58`, clamped to [0,1] by `clamp01` in `competence.ts:44-46`) only when you have a concrete reason to believe the local model improved — e.g. after a fine-tune (`shouldFineTune`, `competence.ts:299-313`) or a model swap. For a one-off re-test, use `:small1` (`forceArchimedes`, `alternator.ts:320-329`) instead: it bypasses the gate deterministically for that session at zero ongoing cost. `aura doctor` surfaces the current rate and which categories are gated (`doctor/checks.ts:497-504`, `:539-553`).

**Bottom line:** the rate is low because a probe is the only path that pays for *both* models plus a verifier on a single task, and because competence scores converge from a 50-episode window — a few samples move the score, so 5% buys the anti-freeze guarantee at roughly 5% of the premium cost instead of 50%.

**Answer: it's a linear ramp from 0.3 to 0.6, based purely on how far the pattern has progressed toward `minAttempts` — success rate is ignored entirely in that branch.**

The code is `aura-code-src/src/archimedes/competence.ts:168-176`:

```ts
if (level.attemptCount < cfg.minAttempts) {
  return safeDecision({
    useArchimedes: true,
    reason: `Only ${level.attemptCount} prior attempt(s) ...`,
    confidence: clamp01(0.3 + level.attemptCount / Math.max(cfg.minAttempts, 1) * 0.3),
    ...
  });
}
```

How the number is built:

1. **`level.attemptCount`** — from `buildLevelFromEpisodes` (line 101-103): the count of *matched* episodes where `archimedesAttempted === true`. Episodes where Archimedes wasn't tried don't count.
2. **`cfg.minAttempts`** — sanitized in `safeConfig` (line 42) to `Math.max(0, config?.minAttempts ?? default)`, so it can never be negative.
3. **The formula** — `0.3 + (attemptCount / minAttempts) * 0.3`:
   - 0 attempts → **0.30**
   - half of `minAttempts` → **0.45**
   - one short of `minAttempts` → just under **0.60**
   - `clamp01` (line 29-32) caps it at 1 and maps `NaN`/`Infinity` to 0.
4. **`Math.max(cfg.minAttempts, 1)`** is the divide-by-zero guard: if `minAttempts` is configured as 0, the branch is unreachable anyway (0 < 0 is false), but the guard keeps the expression safe.

Two consequences worth knowing:

- **Success rate is deliberately not part of this score.** A pattern with 0/9 successes and one with 9/9 successes get the identical confidence at the same attempt count — the "learning phase" is treated as evidence-free by design, and the decision is always `useArchimedes: true` (explore).
- **The ceiling is 0.6**, so a learning-phase decision can never outrank a proven one. Once `attemptCount >= minAttempts`, the score switches to a different formula: `successRate * min(1, attemptCount/10)` (line 182) when the threshold is met, or `(1 - successRate) * min(1, attemptCount/10)` (line 191) when escalating — both of which also scale down for low sample counts.

The `catch` at line 195-201 is the only path that yields `confidence: 0` (assessment error, defaults to trying Archimedes).
